### [DISPUTED] Blue Cross Blue Shield of Massachusetts' "Evaluation and management overcoding program" (dates of service on or after November 3, 2025; commercial, FEP and Medicare Advantage) documents the clearest 835 signature of a downcode in the industry: the submitted E/M line is denied, a new line is added with the reduced code, loop 2300 carries REF*9A*DOWNCODED and the line carries RARC N22; other Blue plans (BCBS Illinois and Texas from July 1, 2026, BCBS Montana, BCBS Alabama, BCBS Tennessee) run claims-only E/M level edits with similar mechanics.

**Corrected (source check):** Blue Cross Blue Shield of Massachusetts' "Evaluation and management overcoding program" (pre-payment claims editing; dates of service on or after November 3, 2025; office/outpatient new and established visits and ED visits; professional claims only; commercial, Federal Employee Program and Medicare Advantage members) is the only payer-published, explicit 835 downcoding indicator found among the sources reviewed. Per BCBSMA's provider Q&A (document MPC_072125-2W-3-QA, revised 8/26): the program compares a provider's frequency of level 4/5 E/M codes "against clinical peers who have similar patient populations and medical decision making complexity"; when those outlier providers' claims "exceed the maximum level of E/M service based on the diagnosis and other claim information, the E/M code is reduced to reflect the maximum level"; "the original E/M code will be denied, and a line will be added with the newly adjusted E/M code"; level 5 may be adjusted to 4 or 3 and level 4 to 3; BCBSMA says it will never fully deny the E/M without adding a lower level and never adjust below level 3; at launch it expected to include 1% to 4% of network PCPs and specialists; claims are reviewed "based on the individual provider identification number, not by the tax ID or group"; it uses "advanced analytics and peer comparison data instead of medical record review", evaluating current claim information and member claim history (and, for ED visits, the facility claim). ERA/835 signature: RARC N22 on the service line (loop 2110, LQ*HE*N22~) plus a claim-level REF segment with qualifier 9A and the value DOWNCODED (REF*9A*DOWNCODED~). BCBSMA labels that REF the "2300 Loop under claim information", but the X12 835 005010X221A1 has no loop 2300: the claim-level REF (9A = Repriced Claim Reference Number) sits in loop 2100 under CLP, so a detection rule should match claim-level REF*9A = DOWNCODED and line-level N22, and should not assume any particular CARC on the denied original line or that SVC06 is populated on the added line, because the Q&A specifies neither. BCBSMA tells providers to share the remittance with their clearinghouse if the indicator is not visible. Payspan Provider Detail Advisories show the "Submitted Procedure" and "Corrected Procedure" codes on the line and message K769 ("SUBMITTED E/M CODE DENIED. LINE ADDED WITH REDUCED E/M CODE BASED ON DIAGNOSTIC INFORMATION. THE INFORMATION SUBMITTED DOES NOT SUPPORT THE LEVEL OF SERVICE."); paper vouchers show K769 and K825 ("A NEW LINE WAS ADDED TO THE CLAIM AND INCLUDES PAYMENT FOR RELATED SERVICES PERFORMED ON THE SAME DATE."). Appeals "with complete medical records" follow the standard Request for Claim Review process and are not reviewed by the analyst who reviewed the original claim (subsequent appeals also go to different analysts); time-based code selection is considered only on appeal; providers are automatically removed when their high-level E/M frequency returns to peer norms "for a sustained period of time (typically six to twelve months)". Other Blue plans run E/M level reviews, but none of their published documents describe comparable 835 mechanics, so no REF*9A/N22-style signature should be assumed for them: Health Care Service Corporation's BCBS Illinois, BCBS Texas and BCBS Montana posted identical notices dated March 16, 2026 stating that for commercial members' dates of service on or after July 1, 2026, "if services billed do not support the level of E&M services billed, your reimbursement will be for a lower level of service validated," following AMA level-of-service and medical decision-making guidelines, with disagreeing providers told they "may submit medical records to support your claim"; the referenced policy (Reimbursement Policy RP024, formerly Clinical Payment and Coding Policy CPCP024, version 1.0, committee approval November 14, 2025, plan effective February 23, 2026) is a generic E/M coding reference (MDM table, time, split/shared, critical care, prolonged services) containing no claim-editing, outlier-selection, downcoding or remittance language. BCBSIL says it "conducts post-service, pre-pay reviews of professional evaluation and management (E/M) claims" and "does not 'automatically downcode' claims or use artificial intelligence" (Behavioral Health Business, 10/7/2026); BCBS Montana gave the same denial (BHB, 8/28/2026); the Chicago Sun-Times (8/26/2026) reports BCBSIL neither confirmed nor denied using an AI tool. Reported impact: an unnamed Chicago psychiatry/psychotherapy practice had 2,400 claims reduced in July 2026 and a suburban pediatrician reported about 40% of higher-level sick-visit claims downcoded (Sun-Times); separately, Illinois Psychiatric Society president Steven Weinstein said one large Illinois group had "roughly 1,400" claims downcoded in July and 2,053 in August, and that 70% to 80% of appeals are being paid (BHB, 10/7/2026); in Montana, Big Sky Psychiatry reported 17% of adult and 39% of pediatric claims downcoded in July 2026, commonly to 99213, at roughly $60 to $110 per claim (BHB, 8/28/2026). Illinois' Transparency in Downcoding Act, signed in 2026, bars automated/AI downcoding and takes effect in 2028. BCBS Alabama: behavioral health providers report downcoding; the plan denies automatic downcoding and says "coding adjustments are made if a clinical review determines a claim was coded incorrectly"; no published BCBS Alabama program notice was found. BCBS Tennessee: a Tennessee Medical Association legal-department alert published by the Memphis Medical Society on 9/23/2025 says BCBST "has algorithmic denials reviewed by nurse coders before the downcode is processed" (the same alert reports Cigna's automatic E/M downcoding effective October 1, 2025 and says Humana, Aetna and BCBS have adopted similar policies); no BCBST policy document was located. None of these plans is an ODA primary payer, and none of the cited sources describes Anthem Kentucky or Kentucky FEP claim editing; BCBSMA's inclusion of FEP members is confirmed but applies to BCBSMA-administered FEP claims.

_Why:_ I opened every cited URL (WebFetch, curl, pdftotext on the two PDFs) and followed the real hyperlinks to the primary HCSC documents. CONFIRMED verbatim from the BCBSMA Q&A PDF (footer "MPC_072125-2W-3-QA (8/26)", matching the "8/26 revision"): Nov 3, 2025 DOS; commercial/FEP/Medicare Advantage; peer-comparison on level 4/5 frequency; "the original E/M code will be denied, and a line will be added with the newly adjusted E/M code"; 5 to 4 or 3, 4 to 3; never fully denied, never below level 3; 1%-4% of PCPs/specialists at launch; review by "individual provider identification number, not by the tax ID or group" (the Q&A does not literally say NPI); "REF*9A*DOWNCODED~ / LQ*HE*N22~"; K769 text; appeals with complete medical records via Request for Claim Review, different analysts; removal after "typically six to twelve months"; time-based coding evaluated only on appeal. The osteopathic.org PDF is BCBSMA's program guidelines (MPC_072125-2W-2-BG, 08/25) and confirms dates and level reductions but has no 835 detail. CONFIRMED: BCBSIL 3/16/2026 notice text (exact quote, commercial, DOS 7/1/2026, RP024, submit medical records); identical BCBSTX and BCBSMT notices dated 3/16/2026 at their ow

BCBSMA Q&A (8/26 revision): compares a provider's level 4/5 frequency against "clinical peers who have similar patient populations and medical decision making complexity"; when claims "exceed the maximum level of E/M service based on the diagnosis and other claim information, the E/M code is reduced"; "the original E/M code will be denied, and a line will be added with the newly adjusted E/M code"; level 5 may go to 4 or 3, level 4 to 3; never fully denied and never below level 3; applies to 1%-4% of PCPs and specialists at launch; reviews by individual provider NPI, not TIN; ERA shows "Remittance Advice Remark Code (RARC) N22... REF*9A*DOWNCODED~ LQ*HE*N22~"; Payspan/voucher message K769 "SUBMITTED E/M CODE DENIED. LINE ADDED WITH REDUCED E/M CODE BASED ON DIAGNOSTIC INFORMATION..."; appeals with complete medical records follow the standard Request for Claim Review process and are reviewed by different analysts; providers are automatically removed when their high-level coding frequency returns to peer norms "for a sustained period of time (typically six to twelve months)"; time-based coding is only evaluated on appeal. BCBSIL notice (3/16/2026): for commercial dates of service from 7/1/2026, "if services billed do not support the level of E&M services billed," reimbursement is for a lower validated level under AMA MDM guidelines; disagreeing providers may submit medical records; policy RP024. BCBSTX: same July 1, 2026 start (Intellicure, 5/29/2026; policy CPCP024). Chicago Sun-Times (8/26/2026) and Behavioral Health Business (10/7/2026): one Illinois group had 2,400 claims reduced in July 2026 and 2,053 in August; BCBSIL says it conducts "post-service, pre-pay reviews" and denies automatic downcoding or AI; Illinois Psychiatric Society reports 70%-80% of appeals being paid; BCBS Montana announced E/M claim-editing changes in March 2026. TMA alert (9/23/2025): BCBS Tennessee routes algorithmic flags to nurse coders before downcoding. BCBSMA's program explicitly includes FEP members.

**What it means for ODA:** Implement the BCBSMA-style parser rule generically: a downcode is present when (a) an E/M line is denied and a second line for a lower-level E/M with the same date/provider appears, (b) REF*9A contains DOWNCODED, or (c) LQ*HE*N22 appears on an E/M line, in addition to SVC06-vs-SVC01 mismatches. Because the industry is converging on 'never below level 3' and 'remove after 6-12 months of normalized coding', the dashboard should report each provider's level 4/5 share versus specialty peers and the months since last downcode. Also check whether out-of-area Blue members' claims (BlueCard) adjudicated by home plans such as BCBSIL/BCBSTX appear in ODA's 835s with these signatures.

Sources: [provider.bluecrossma.com](https://provider.bluecrossma.com/ProviderHome/wcm/connect/72220b4e-97de-4e0b-8f24-0759f12a3e92/MPC_072125-2W-5-QA+EM+overcoding+Q&As.pdf?MOD=AJPERES) · [osteopathic.org](https://osteopathic.org/wp-content/uploads/Overcoding-Program-Guidelines.pdf) · [bcbsil.com](https://www.bcbsil.com/provider/education/education-reference/news/2026/3-16-2026-claim-editing-changes-for-evaluation-and-management-services) · [intellicure.com](https://www.intellicure.com/blog/downcode-em-services/) · [chicago.suntimes.com](https://chicago.suntimes.com/health/2026/08/26/blue-cross-blue-shield-lowers-payment-therapists-pediatricians-new-downcoding-policy) · [bhbusiness.com](https://bhbusiness.com/2026/10/07/bcbs-downcoding-issues-impact-behavioral-health-providers-in-multiple-states/) · [mdmemphis.org](https://www.mdmemphis.org/member-alert-beware-of-automatic-downcoding-by-health-insurers/) · [bcbsil.com](https://www.bcbsil.com/docs/provider/il/standards/cpcp/2026/cpcp024-2-23-2026.pdf) · [bcbstx.com](https://www.bcbstx.com/provider/education/education/news/2026/3-16-2026-claim-editing-changes-for-evaluation-and-management-services) · [bcbstx.com](https://www.bcbstx.com/docs/provider/tx/standards/clinical-pay-coding/2026/cpcp024-2-23-2026.pdf)

### [UNVERIFIED] Official X12 code definitions needed for downcoding detection: CARC 150 "Payer deems the information submitted does not support this level of service"; CARC 186 "Level of care change adjustment"; CARC B10 "Allowed amount has been reduced because a component of the basic procedure/test was paid"; CARC 59 "Processed based on multiple or concurrent procedure rules"; CARC 45 "Charge exceeds fee schedule/maximum allowable or contracted/legislated fee arrangement"; CARC 252 "An attachment/other documentation is required to adjudicate this claim/service"; CARC 187 consumer spending account payments; CARC 94 "Processed in Excess of charges"; RARC N22 (Alert) "This procedure code was added/changed because it more accurately describes the services rendered"; M80 "Not covered when performed during the same session/date as a previously processed service"; N431 "Not covered with this procedure"; N463 "Missing support data for claim"; N657 "This should be billed with the appropriate code for these services"; M25 "The information furnished does not substantiate the need for this level of service"; the AMA's downcoding resource adds M85 "Subjected to review of physician evaluation and management services" and N610 "Alert: Payment based on appropriate level of care," and warns that some payers "pay at the adjusted lower rate without changing the billed code."

Definitions extracted from the X12 external code lists (x12.org) on 2026-11-06. AMA "Payer evaluation and management (E/M) downcoding programs: what you need to know" (2022, still the AMA's current resource per BOT Report 11-I-26): example remark codes CO150, M85, N610, CARC 186; "certain payers even pay at the adjusted lower rate without changing the billed code, making it extremely difficult to identify in coding audits"; practices should compare payment for each billed CPT to the anticipated amount and watch 99204/99205/99214/99215 and modifier 25 claims. AMA Resolution 815 (I-26) notes that downcoded claims "are processed and posted as paid rather than denied," so they bypass denial queues, and asks that billing systems "automatically detect from the electronic remittance advice when a payer has adjudicated or reimbursed a claim at an E/M service level lower than the level submitted — whether by code reassignment or payment reduction." Payer-specific mappings observed: Anthem CO-186 (forum reports 2021 and Jan 2026); BCBSMA N22 + REF*9A*DOWNCODED; Cigna, Aetna, Humana and UHC remittance codes for E/M level adjustments were not published in any source read. ISMS survey (cited in AMA BOT Report 11-I-26): only 16% of physicians said their remittance usually or always indicates a downcode, and 72% said the remit rarely or never gives a specific reason.

**What it means for ODA:** The detection engine needs four independent signals, because payers are inconsistent: (1) SVC06 submitted code differs from SVC01 paid code (any lower E/M level, or an added modifier such as 51); (2) CAS carrying CARC 150, 186, B10 (on E/M-25 lines), 252/N463 (records demand) or RARC N22/M25/M85/N610; (3) a denied E/M line paired with a newly added lower-level E/M line on the same claim; (4) 'silent' downcodes where the code is unchanged but AMT*B6 equals the payer's known allowed for the next-lower level (requires a per-payer, per-code allowed-amount reference built from ODA's own clean lines, e.g., BCBS 99213 $51.00 vs 99214 $63.89). Each flagged line should carry the payer's CARC/RARC text verbatim into the appeal packet; billers must be stopped from posting CARC 186/150 amounts as CO-45 contractual write-offs.

Sources: [x12.org](https://x12.org/codes/claim-adjustment-reason-codes) · [x12.org](https://x12.org/codes/remittance-advice-remark-codes) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/payer-em-downcoding-resource.pdf) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/i26-handbook-refcomm-j.pdf) · [aapc.com](https://www.aapc.com/discuss/threads/anthem-downcoding.181067/)

### [UNVERIFIED] The AMA has formal policy opposing claims-only downcoding (Policy D-320.972, adopted as Res. 714, A-24) and, per its November 2026 Board of Trustees Report 11-I-26, has written to Aetna (July 2024), Cigna (August 2025), AHIP and the Blue Cross Blue Shield Association (March 2025) without success, adopted a state model bill ("Transparency in Downcoding Act," June 2025), and publishes an appeal-letter template; CPT's own E/M guidelines state that "The final diagnosis for a condition does not, in and of itself, determine the complexity or risk."

Policy D-320.972: the AMA "vigorously opposes health plans using software, algorithms, or methodologies, other than manual review of the patient's medical record, to deny or downcode evaluation and management services, except correct coding protocol denials, based solely on the CPT/HCPCS codes, ICD-10 codes, and/or modifiers submitted on the claim," and supports clear CARC/RARC notice when a downcode follows record review. Policy D-70.940 (Res. 810, I-25) directs regulatory, legislative and legal challenges. BOT Report 11-I-26: "plans have largely refused to voluntarily rescind their downcoding programs"; model bill prohibits automatic downcoding without documented clinical record review, prohibits downcoding based solely on diagnosis codes, requires CARCs/RARCs to notify and explain downcodes, mandates a disclosed appeal process, permits batching of similarly downcoded claims, prohibits targeting physicians treating complex patients, and sets penalties including claim reprocessing orders; ISMS survey: 72% experienced automatic downcoding of appropriately coded claims, 77% say it increased in five years, payers uphold 53% of challenged reductions; AMA Congressional Affairs is seeking a federal bill. AMA PPPS Resolution 9 (I-25) named Cigna's R49 and Aetna's CCRP. Resolution 816 (I-26) cites the Maryland order and asks CMS to enforce CMS-4201-F against MA plans; it quotes an estimate (vendor-sourced, unverified) of $28,000-$74,000 per family physician per year lost to algorithmic downcoding. AMA sample appeal letter (resource appendix) frames the request as reimbursement of the billed code because "Downcoding of CPT E/M codes is not appropriate without review of medical record documentation." The AAFP, AAO-HNS, AAAAI, ACR, ASGE, APMA, AASM, LUGPA, CSRO and AAO sent letters in 2025; AASM also wrote UnitedHealthcare, Anthem, Humana, BCBS NC/MA/TN, Ambetter, Kaiser, Molina, WellCare and Sunshine Health. No AAD/AADA letter on E/M downcoding was found.

**What it means for ODA:** Appeal-packet generator should embed: the CPT guideline sentence on final diagnosis, the AMA D-320.972 position, the payer's own policy language (R49 reinstatement clause, Humana CP2024003, Anthem's 'disputes must include documentation'), the submitted-vs-paid code and dollar delta, and the MDM/time elements highlighted in the note; batch similar claims per payer where the payer permits. The dashboard should also export de-identified downcode statistics for the AMA's downcoding survey, KMA and the AADA, because regulator and society pressure is what has produced pauses and orders.

Sources: [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/i26-handbook-refcomm-j.pdf) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/i25-ppps-resolution-9.pdf) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/payer-em-downcoding-resource.pdf) · [aaaai.org](https://www.aaaai.org/Aaaai/media/Media-Library-PDFs/About/Advocacy/CIGNA_Downcoding_letter_18sep2025-IV.pdf) · [aasm.org](https://aasm.org/growing-trend-of-downcoding-among-commercial-payers/) · [nbcnews.com](https://www.nbcnews.com/news/amp/rcna230714)

### [UNVERIFIED] State action in 2025-2026 created enforceable levers: the Maryland Insurance Administration's Consent Order MIA-2026-03-009 (March 13, 2026) fined Cigna $80,000, ordered it to stop automatically downcoding E/M claims and to reprocess affected claims, and MIA Bulletin 26-9 (April 7, 2026) extended the prohibition to all Maryland health insurers; Indiana enacted HB 1271 (signed March 2026) requiring notice of downcodes, barring diagnosis-only downcoding and AI as the sole basis, requiring a clear appeals process that permits batching, and barring targeting of physicians who treat complex patients; Illinois SB 3114 ("Transparency in Downcoding Act", signed summer 2026, effective 2028) and Virginia SB 164/HB 484 (signed April 2026) impose similar rules; California regulators paused Cigna's and Anthem's policies; no Kentucky downcoding statute or Department of Insurance bulletin was found.

Maryland: Oncology Practice Management (July 2026) names Consent Order MIA-2026-03-009, 3/13/2026, $80,000, cease automatic downcoding, reprocess claims, and Bulletin 26-9 of 4/7/2026; AASM (4/10/2026) and AMA (3/27/2026 state update; BOT Report 11-I-26) confirm the fine and order and that MIA found reductions "in violation of Maryland's insurance statutes governing claim payment and processing"; AMA Resolution 816 characterizes the finding as applying a policy rule "before any human reviewed the encounter." Indiana: AMA BOT Report 11-I-26: "in March 2026... Indiana's Governor signed legislation" with the provisions listed; engrossed HB 1271 text ties the new chapter to July 1, 2026 and defines downcoding as an insurer's unilateral alteration of the payment for or level of an E/M or other service code resulting in lower payment; an unofficial tracker lists it as Public Law 88 (unverified); the law also bars providers from submitting AI-generated claims without clinician review. Illinois: Sun-Times (8/26/2026) and BHB (10/7/2026): Gov. Pritzker signed SB 3114, effective January 2028, barring automated processes/AI to reduce physician claims, requiring notice with clinical reason, and barring discrimination against physicians treating complex patients; ISMS survey found 72% of practices experienced automatic downcoding. Virginia: AMA BOT report: signed April 2026, limits downcoding based solely on CPT and diagnosis, requires detailed explanations and batch appeals. NBC (10/2025): Arkansas and Virginia passed transparency laws in 2025; Ohio SB 165, New Jersey S594 and Connecticut SB 817 stalled. California: Cigna R49 paused for DMHC-regulated fully insured HMO products from 10/1/2025 and CDI-regulated fully insured PPO products from 11/9/2025 (30-day review), not self-insured plans; Anthem's policy paused through at least 9/1/2026. Tennessee (TMA 9/23/2025): downcoded claims are not treated as clean claims and no prompt-pay interest accrues during reconsideration. Kentucky: searches of the 2026 General Assembly record and Department of Insurance bulletins found no downcoding measure; KMA's 2019 letter invoked KRS 304.17A-235 (material-change notice, described as 90 days) but the statute text was not retrieved.

**What it means for ODA:** Kentucky-regulated plans are not yet bound by a downcoding statute, so ODA's leverage is contractual, regulatory complaint (Kentucky DOI unfair claims settlement / prompt-pay) and the payers' own published dispute rules; but ODA's Indiana-regulated commercial patients (Anthem Indiana, Medicare of Indiana region employers) can invoke HB 1271's notice, no-diagnosis-only and batch-appeal rights for dates of service on or after its effective date, and the Maryland order is persuasive evidence in any appeal that Cigna's R49 methodology has been found unlawful by a regulator. The dashboard should tag each claim's regulating jurisdiction (state of the policy and self-funded vs fully insured, since state laws generally do not reach self-funded ERISA plans) and the legal lens should verify KRS 304.17A-235 and Kentucky prompt-pay interest.

Sources: [oncpracticemanagement.com](https://www.oncpracticemanagement.com/issues/2026/july-2026-vol-16-no-4/downcoding-in-the-oncology-practice-how-to-identify-it-and-how-to-fix-it) · [aasm.org](https://aasm.org/states-challenge-private-insurer-em-downcoding/) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/health-care-advocacy/advocacy-update/march-27-2026-state-advocacy-update) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/i26-handbook-refcomm-j.pdf) · [chicago.suntimes.com](https://chicago.suntimes.com/health/2026/08/26/blue-cross-blue-shield-lowers-payment-therapists-pediatricians-new-downcoding-policy) · [bhbusiness.com](https://bhbusiness.com/2026/10/07/bcbs-downcoding-issues-impact-behavioral-health-providers-in-multiple-states/) · [cmadocs.org](https://www.cmadocs.org/newsroom/news/view/update-cigna-clarifies-scope-of-downcoding-policy-pause) · [cmadocs.org](https://www.cmadocs.org/newsroom/news/view/ArticleId/50993/Cigna-agrees-to-pause-controversial-downcoding-policy) · [entnet.org](https://www.entnet.org/resource/downcoding-reform-state-legislative-advocacy-efforts/) · [mdmemphis.org](https://www.mdmemphis.org/member-alert-beware-of-automatic-downcoding-by-health-insurers/)

### [UNVERIFIED] Medicare Advantage downcoding is now a top-five administrative burden nationally (MGMA 2026 Regulatory Burden Report, April 9, 2026), MGMA is circulating a draft federal bill to bar automatic downcoding by MA plans (September 2026), and while 42 CFR 422.101(b) obliges MA organizations to follow Traditional Medicare coverage criteria, NCDs and LCDs, paragraph (b) does not address payment levels or claims editing, so the CMS-4201-F argument against MA E/M downcoding is contested rather than settled.

MGMA press release: survey of leaders from more than 230 group practices; "Three of the top five administrative challenges are tied to MA: prior authorization, claim denials, and automatic downcoding"; 90% report a shift toward MA and 79% of those report negative operational impact; Anders Gilberg: "excessive denials and automatic downcoding are delaying care." Medical Economics (9/28/2026): MGMA's Madison Hynes says downcoding is "the biggest increase that we're hearing from members on"; the clean draft bars MA plans from automatic downcoding; the fallback permits it only after a plan proves upcoding above a set rate, with notice, for no more than 90 days. 42 CFR 422.101(b)(2): MA organizations must comply with general coverage and benefit conditions in Traditional Medicare laws, including criteria for determining whether an item or service is a Traditional Medicare benefit; (b)(6) governs publicly accessible internal coverage criteria when Medicare criteria are not fully established (Cornell LII summary). AMA Resolution 816 (I-26) asserts CMS-4201-F requires MA plans to follow Traditional Medicare billing standards and asks CMS to enforce it against algorithmic downcoding; the regulation text itself addresses coverage, not E/M payment levels. Traditional Medicare (CGS J15/J8) has no automatic E/M downcoding program; MA plans' diagnosis-based E/M edits (Humana CP2024003, Elevance's MA program) are internal edits not used in Traditional Medicare.

**What it means for ODA:** For Humana MA, UHC MA and Anthem MA appeals, the strongest framing is factual (documentation meets CPT MDM/time; Traditional Medicare pays these levels without diagnosis-based reduction) rather than a bare citation of CMS-4201-F; the dashboard should report MA downcode rates side by side with the practice's Traditional Medicare paid-level distribution (CGS J15, Medicare Indiana J8) as the benchmark, and quantify MA-vs-FFS allowed-amount gaps for 99213/99214 (e.g., observed Humana MA 99213 $66.64 and UHC MA $76.64 versus Medicare KY $70.81-$83.30) to support complaints to CMS and MGMA/AMA advocacy.

Sources: [prnewswire.com](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/new-mgma-2026-burden-report-finds-increased-regulatory-and-administrative-burden-drive-physician-burnout-threatening-patient-access-302738505.html) · [medicaleconomics.com](https://www.medicaleconomics.com/view/mgma-shops-draft-bill-to-curb-automatic-downcoding-in-medicare-advantage) · [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/42/422.101) · [ama-assn.org](https://www.ama-assn.org/system/files/i26-handbook-refcomm-j.pdf)

### [UNVERIFIED] CMS finalized (CY2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule, reported November 5, 2026) that when a separately identifiable office/outpatient E/M visit is billed on the same day as a procedure with a 0-day global period, Medicare will pay 100% for the higher-cost service and 80% for the second service (the proposed 50% reduction and the inclusion of 10- and 90-day globals were dropped); the AADA estimates an approximately 6% overall Medicare payment reduction for dermatologists in 2027 and has argued that private payers and MA plans will copy the policy.

Dermatology Times (11/5/2026): final policy applies only to 0-day global procedures; 100%/80% payment; AADA: "we are disappointed that CMS chose to move forward with policies that will harm patients"; ~6% estimate also reflects conversion factor and practice expense changes. A 9/10/2026 comment letter to CMS Administrator Oz (regulations.gov, search snippet) cites an AADA-commissioned analysis of the proposed 50% cut and an Academy analysis putting dermatology's modifier 25 use on office visits at about 61.7% in CY2025, and argues MA and commercial payers would mirror the policy. This is the only 2025-2026 AADA advocacy item on same-day E/M payment found; no AADA statement on commercial E/M level downcoding was located.

**What it means for ODA:** From 2027 dates of service, Medicare, and likely MA and commercial remits, will show reduced allowed amounts on the lower-cost line when an E/M-25 is billed with a 0-day global procedure (common dermatology pairs such as biopsies 11102-11107 and destruction codes with 0-day globals); the detection engine must distinguish this lawful 20% reduction (expect CARC 59 or a payer-specific code on the second line) from improper modifier 25 denials and from level downcodes, and should model the dollar impact per provider so ODA can decide which same-day pairings to keep, reschedule, or appeal. Verify the final rule text and effective date (January 1, 2027) and the exact CARC Medicare will use when the rule's claims-processing instructions are published.

Sources: [dermatologytimes.com](https://www.dermatologytimes.com/view/aada-warns-of-6-medicare-payment-cut-for-dermatologists-in-2027)

### [UNVERIFIED] Published appeal/reconsideration windows for ODA's payers: Cigna, 180 calendar days from the initial payment or denial decision, or from the last payment adjustment for adjusted claims (single-level payment review, decision within 60 days); Aetna, reconsideration within 180 calendar days of the claim decision and appeal within 60 calendar days of the reconsideration decision; Humana, claim disputes within 18 months of the paid date (all products) via Availity; UnitedHealthcare, a combined 12-month reconsideration-plus-appeal window from the original EOB/PRA according to secondary summaries of the 2026 Care Provider Administrative Guide (unverified); Anthem, 60 calendar days from the remittance advice for the first-step reconsideration in Indiana, and 365 days from date of service or 60 days from the EOP (whichever is later) in Ohio, with the Kentucky commercial window not confirmed.

Cigna: the Cigna provider appeal policy page (static.cigna.com, text seen in search results; direct fetch returned no content) states the payment review must be requested "within 180 calendar days of the initial payment or denial decision" and, for adjusted payments, "within 180 calendar days of the date of the last payment adjustment," with notification "within 75 business days of receipt of the original dispute"; R49 reconsiderations go through CignaforHCP or fax 833.392.2092. Aetna: disputes overview page (read) as quoted. Humana: 2025 Provider Manual text quoted in the Humana finding. UHC: policy web page returned 404; a 2025 UHC Wisconsin quick reference guide describes 60-day reconsideration windows, while 2026 third-party guides describe 12 months; participation agreement controls. Anthem: Anthem Indiana claims page (search snippet) "must be completed within 60 calendar days of the date of the provider's remittance advice"; Anthem Ohio reference guide (search snippet) 365 days from DOS or 60 days from EOP; Availity is Anthem's preferred channel (Claims & Payments > Disputes). Several payers (Anthem via Availity, Aetna via Availity, Humana via Availity 'Dispute Claim') accept disputes with attachments electronically; Cigna R49 reconsiderations also accept fax.

**What it means for ODA:** The dashboard's deadline engine should compute, per flagged line, days remaining using the payer's window from the 835 payment date (BPR16/CLP) with these defaults, flag Anthem (60 days) and UHC/Aetna/Cigna (180 days or 12 months) items as urgent, and treat Humana's 18-month window as the backlog-recovery opportunity; the Kentucky Anthem commercial window and the UHC 2026 guide must be confirmed against ODA's contracts before the first batch of appeals.

Sources: [aetna.com](https://www.aetna.com/health-care-professionals/disputes-appeals/disputes-appeals-overview.html) · [assets.humana.com](https://assets.humana.com/is/content/humana/FINAL589003ALL1024_2025_ProviderManualNonDelegatedpdf) · [providernewsroom.com](https://providernewsroom.com/cigna-healthcare/new-reimbursement-policy-for-professional-evaluation-and-management-services-claims-effective-october-1-2025/) · [cigna.com](https://www.cigna.com/health-care-providers/coverage-and-claims/appeals-disputes) · [anthem.com](https://www.anthem.com/in/provider/state-federal/claims/submissions-disputes) · [providers.anthem.com](https://providers.anthem.com/docs/gpp/OH_CAID_ClaimsEscalation.pdf)

### Open questions

- Is Cigna R49 still being applied outside California and Maryland as of November 2026? Cigna's Jan-Oct 2026 monthly policy bulletins show no revision or retirement, Part B News (1/22/2026) said it was in effect, but a July 2026 trade article says it ran about five months; ODA's Cigna 835s for Oct 2025 onward should be mined for one-level reductions to settle this.
- Has Anthem Blue Cross Blue Shield Kentucky (commercial, Anthem Medicare Advantage, and Anthem-administered FEP) issued a 2025/2026 notice equivalent to the California November 13, 2025 automatic E/M downcoding policy, and is the May 2023 Kentucky Medicare Advantage restart of the diagnosis-weighted program still active? Anthem's Kentucky provider news archive was not retrievable.
- Is Anthem Kentucky's February 2019 policy (deny E/M-25 on the day of a related procedure when a same/similar-diagnosis service occurred within ~60 days) still in force, and what are the exact Anthem Kentucky commercial and Medicare Advantage reconsideration deadlines (Indiana says 60 days from the remittance advice; Ohio says 365 days from DOS or 60 days from EOP)?
- Which CARC/RARC combinations do Cigna (R49), Aetna, Humana (CP2024003) and UHC/UMR actually emit on 835s for E/M level reductions? None were published; Anthem CO-186 and BCBSMA N22 + REF*9A*DOWNCODED are the only documented signatures. ODA's own files (SVC06 vs SVC01 mismatches, CAS codes on those lines) are the best source.
- What UMR edit produced PI-B10 on 99214-25 billed with 17110, and do UMR, UnitedHealthcare Shared Services, Surest and Optum-administered plans apply UHC's commercial E/M, Rebundling (G2211) and modifier 25 policies verbatim? UMR's disclosure says UnitedHealth affiliate payment policies 'may' apply; no UMR-specific policy text was found.
- Does Humana apply an E/M level policy to commercial members (the only published policy, CP2024003, is Medicare Advantage), has CP2024003 been revised since 04/2024, and does any vendor (Cotiviti or other) run the review? The claim that Humana expanded predictive-analytics downcoding to commercial and MA in 2025 is from a single trade article.
- Did Aetna extend its E&M Claim and Code Review Program to Medicare Advantage in late 2025 (ISMA said it was planned; Aetna told APMA in June 2025 that no MA claims were adjusted; CSRO's July 2025 letter was addressed to Aetna Medicare Advantage)? Relevant only if ODA sees Aetna remits.
- Are Anthem FEP claims subject to Anthem's E/M program or modifier reviews? BCBSMA explicitly includes FEP members in its program, but no FEP-specific Anthem statement was found; FEP disputes may follow OPM-governed rules.
- Final enacted text, Public Law number and effective date of Indiana HB 1271 (engrossed text points to July 1, 2026; an unofficial tracker says Public Law 88), and whether it reaches self-funded ERISA plans (state laws generally do not).
- Exact text of KRS 304.17A-235 (notice of material changes to provider reimbursement, cited by KMA as requiring 90 days' notice) and Kentucky prompt-pay interest rules, for use as Kentucky-specific levers; no Kentucky downcoding statute or DOI bulletin was found.
- The precise reconsideration/appeal window in UnitedHealthcare's 2026 Care Provider Administrative Guide (secondary sources say 12 months combined; a 2025 Wisconsin guide says 60 days) and ODA's participation-agreement overrides for each payer.
- Whether BlueCard claims for out-of-area Blue members (e.g., BCBSIL, BCBSTX, BCBSMA) appear in ODA's 835s with the home plan's E/M downcoding signatures, and whether Anthem KY applies home-plan edits as host.
- The exact CY2027 Medicare final-rule text for the same-day E/M (modifier 25) 80% payment policy, its effective date, and the CARC/RARC Medicare contractors (CGS) will use, so the detection engine can separate lawful 20% reductions from improper denials.

## Legal and regulatory levers in Kentucky, Indiana and federal law

Research lens: legal/regulatory levers (Kentucky, Indiana, federal). Note: the run's shared WebSearch budget was already exhausted when this agent started, so every finding below was built by reading primary sources directly by URL (Kentucky LRC statute PDFs and KAR pages, Cornell LII CFR text, CMS manuals/guidance PDFs, CGS J15 pages, tricare.mil, DOL FAQs, Texas Legislature). Out-of-state downcoding statutes and Kentucky DOI enforcement actions could not be verified and are listed as open questions.

Key levers found: (1) Kentucky's prompt-pay chapter (KRS 304.17A-700 to -730) requires insured plans to pay or give written reasons within 30 days, limits "contesting" a clean claim to four enumerated grounds (preexisting/COB, retrospective review, fraud, unpaid premium), imposes automatic tiered interest of 12/18/21 percent, bars insurers from making providers "appeal" payments not made at the contracted rate (30-day correction duty, KRS 304.17A-708), caps retroactive denials and recoupments at 24 months with a 30-day dispute right that freezes recoupment (KRS 304.17A-714), and is backed by DOI fines of $1,000/day or 10 percent of the unpaid claim per day (KRS 304.99-123); KRS 304.17A-577 and -235 force disclosure of multi-code payment methodology and 90-day/15-day notice of fee-schedule and claim-edit changes. (2) Indiana has an explicit anti-downcoding statute for insurers and HMOs (IC 27-8-5.7-6.5; IC 27-13-36.2-4.5): a payer may not alter or pay a lower-value CPT code unless the code violates coding rules/contract terms or a human has reviewed the medical record; Indiana prompt pay is 30 days electronic/45 paper with interest. (3) For self-funded (UMR-type) plans, ERISA 29 CFR 2560.503-1 and 2590.715-2719 plus DOL FAQ C-12 make any partial payment an "adverse benefit determination" that triggers a 180-day appeal right, free access to the specific internal rule/edit relied on and all "relevant" documents, and deemed exhaustion if the plan does not strictly comply. (4) For Medicare Advantage, 42 CFR 422.101(b)-(c) (CMS-4201-F) bars coverage criteria not grounded in Traditional Medicare or publicly posted internal criteria; non-contracted providers get Subpart M appeals with a Waiver of Liability within 60 days of receipt (65 days from remit per CMS guidance), and CMS's July 6, 2026 appeals guidance expressly lists "Downcoding" and "level of care or rate of payment" as appealable partial-payment determinations; contracted providers must use the contract dispute process. (5) Medicare FFS (CGS J15): 120-day redetermination; 1-year clerical-error reopening via myCGS for procedure code/modifier/units errors, but "third party payer errors" and unbilled services are excluded. (6) FEP disputes run through 5 CFR 890.105/.107 (6 months to carrier, 90 days to OPM, suit only against OPM by Dec 31 of the 3rd year, provider needs the member's specific written consent). (7) TRICARE: participating providers may appeal within 90 days if $50 or more is in dispute, but the allowable-charge amount itself is non-appealable (32 CFR 199.10). (8) Trend evidence: Texas SB 815 (eff. Sept 1, 2025) bars utilization-review agents from using automated decision systems to make adverse determinations.

### [UNVERIFIED] Kentucky's prompt-pay statute requires an insurer (or any entity processing claims for it) to pay a clean claim, or send written/electronic notice denying or contesting it, within 30 calendar days of receipt, and when it pays less than billed it must pay the undisputed portion and state the reasons the remainder will not be paid; an insurer may 'contest' a clean claim only on four enumerated grounds, none of which is 'we re-leveled the E/M code.'

KRS 304.17A-702(1): 'each insurer shall reimburse a provider for a clean claim or send a written or an electronic notice denying or contesting the claim within thirty (30) calendar days from the date that the claim is received by the insurer or any entity that administers or processes claims on behalf of the insurer.' 702(2): within that time the insurer shall '(a) Pay the total amount of the claim in accordance with any contract between the insurer and the provider; (b) Pay the portion of the claim that is not in dispute and notify the provider, in writing or electronically, of the reasons the remaining portion of the claim will not be paid; or (c) Notify the provider ... of the reasons no part of the claim will be paid.' KRS 304.17A-700(3)(c): a clean claim for non-institutional providers is 'the HCFA 1500 data set or its successor submitted on the designated paper or electronic format as adopted by the National Uniform Claims Committee' plus required attachments; 700(1) 'Adjudicate' means pays, contests, or denies. KRS 304.17A-704(1)(a): electronic claims must be acknowledged within 48 hours; 704(2): at acknowledgment the insurer must identify 'all information that is missing ..., any errors ..., or ... any other circumstances which preclude it from being a clean claim'; 704(3): a corrected clean claim must be adjudicated within the 30-day frame. KRS 304.17A-706(1): 'An insurer may contest a clean claim only in the following instances: (a) ... preexisting condition, coordination of benefits ..., or that another insurer is primarily responsible ...; (b) The insurer will conduct a retrospective review of the services identified on the claim; (c) The insurer has information that the claim was submitted fraudulently; or (d) The covered person's or group's premium has not been paid.' 706(2)(c): if a retrospective review needs an attachment not listed in the provider manual, the insurer must notify the provider within the 30-day frame of the service to be reviewed and the specific information needed, 'Complete the retrospective review within twenty (20) business days of the insurer's receipt of the medical information,' and add interest at 12% per annum (or per 304.17A-730); 706(2)(d): no interest if the provider does not respond within 15 business days. 806 KAR 17:360 Section 3: the payment date is 'The posting date of an electronic payment to a provider account'; the 30 days 'Include[s] the time a claim is with a health care clearinghouse acting on behalf of an insurer'; Section 5(2): an insurer contesting a claim 'shall provide this information to a provider upon request.' KRS 304.17A-726: claims and provider contracts 'shall conform to KRS 304.17A-700 to 304.17A-730' and 'An insurer shall not request or require a provider to pursue any other course of action regarding the payment of health care claims outside of the provisions set forth in' those sections. Scope caveat: 'Insurer' takes the KRS 304.17A-005 meaning; KRS 304.17A-600(9)(c) shows Kentucky reaches only self-insurers 'not exempt from state regulation by ERISA,' so self-funded ERISA plans (e.g., many UMR-administered groups) are outside this statute.

**What it means for ODA:** Detection rules for Kentucky-insured payers (Anthem KY commercial, Humana commercial, UHC fully insured, BCBS): (1) compute days from clearinghouse acceptance (TriZetto 999/277CA date) to 835 BPR16 payment date; >30 days = prompt-pay violation; (2) a line where SVC01 differs from SVC06 (or the submitted CPT from the 837) with only CAS*CO*45 and no remark/notice stating why the submitted code was not paid is a 304.17A-702(2)(b) notice defect; (3) a code substitution that was not preceded by a records request is not a permitted 'contest' under 706(1) unless the payer performed a retrospective review, which itself triggers the 20-business-day and 12% interest rules. Appeal/demand letters should quote 702(2)(a) ('Pay the total amount of the claim in accordance with any contract') and 726 (insurer cannot force a different process). The dashboard needs a per-payer 'KY-regulated vs ERISA self-funded vs federal' flag (data gap: not derivable from the 835 alone).

### [UNVERIFIED] Kentucky imposes automatic, tiered late-payment interest (12%, 18%, 21% per annum) that the insurer must add without the provider asking, and the Department of Insurance may fine an insurer $1,000 per day or 10% of the unpaid claim amount, whichever is greater, for each day a clean claim remains unpaid, plus up to $10,000 for willful or repeated violations; providers can file a 'Provider Complaint' with the DOI Consumer Protection Division, and insurers must answer the DOI within 15 calendar days.

KRS 304.17A-730(1): interest 'on the amount of the claim that remains unpaid' at '(a) ... twelve percent (12%) per annum' for claims paid 1-30 days after the due date, '(b) ... eighteen percent (18%)' for 31-60 days, '(c) ... twenty-one percent (21%)' for more than 60 days, accruing from the date payment was due under 304.17A-702. 730(2): 'the insurer shall add the interest payable to the amount of the unpaid claim without the necessity for any claim for that interest to be made by the provider filing the original claim' (force-majeure exception). 806 KAR 17:360 Section 4(3): 'an insurer shall pay the interest required by KRS 304.17A-730 within thirty (30) days after the date a claim is paid'; 4(4): no interest on corrected payments made under 304.17A-708(1). KRS 304.99-123(1): DOI 'may assess ... (a) A fine of one thousand dollars ($1,000) per day or ten percent (10%) of the unpaid claim amount, whichever is greater, for each day that a clean claim remains unpaid'; '(b) ... a fine of up to ten thousand dollars ($10,000) where the commissioner determines that an insurer has willfully and knowingly violated ... or has a pattern of repeated violations'; 99-123(2): safe harbor when 95% of clean claims per quarter are adjudicated on time and at least 90% of clean-claim dollars not denied/contested are paid on time. KRS 304.17A-722(1)-(3): insurers must report quarterly prompt-pay statistics (counts, percentages by lateness bucket, interest paid) to DOI within 180 days of quarter end; 722(4): DOI 'shall, as part of the market conduct survey of each insurer, audit the insurer to determine compliance ... Findings shall be made available to the public upon request'; 722(5): annual report to the Interim Joint Committee on Banking and Insurance including the number of complaints received and acted upon. KRS 304.17A-724: the chapter applies 'to any entity an insurer contracts with to perform claims processing functions.' KY DOI complaints page: 'A provider Complaint is filed by a medical provider against a health insurance company'; a consumer complaint may be filed by 'a consumer, provider or their representative'; complaints must be in writing (online form on insurance.ky.gov home page, mail, or fax); DOI instructions (July 2024): the insurer has '15 calendar days to provide the Department with a response' and 'the majority of cases are processed and completed within 30 days'; contact: Consumer Protection Division, 500 Mero Street, 2 SE 11, Frankfort, KY 40601, 502-564-6034, DOI.ConsumerComplaints@ky.gov. No 2024-2026 DOI enforcement actions against Anthem, Humana or UHC could be located on the DOI site (see open questions).

**What it means for ODA:** Dashboard dollar model: for every Kentucky-insured claim paid late, compute statutory interest by tier (12/18/21%) on the unpaid amount from day 31, and separately for every uncorrected downcode/underpayment compute interest on the shortfall from the original due date; show 'interest owed but not remitted' per payer (835s rarely carry it; look for separate interest payments or PLB entries). Build a per-payer, per-quarter 'safe-harbor test' from ODA's own claims (percent adjudicated within 30 days; percent of dollars paid within 30 days) to attach to a DOI Provider Complaint when a payer falls below 95%/90%. Generate the DOI complaint packet automatically (claim list with received date, payment date, amount, interest due) and file an open-records request for the payer's market-conduct prompt-pay findings under 304.17A-722(4). Also apply the chapter to delegated processors/clearinghouses under 304.17A-724 (e.g., fee-deducting payment vendors).

### [UNVERIFIED] Under Kentucky law an insurer may not make a provider 'appeal' a payment that was not made at the contracted rate: miscalculations must be corrected and paid within 30 days of the provider's documentation; retroactive denials and recoupments are limited to 24 months after payment, require itemized written notice, and recoupment is frozen while the provider disputes within 30 days; knowing violations are unfair claims settlement practices, as is refusing to pay without a reasonable investigation or failing to explain the basis for a denial.

KRS 304.17A-708(1): 'An insurer shall not require a provider to appeal errors in payment where the insurer has not paid the claim according to the contracted rate. Miscalculations in payments made by the insurer shall be corrected and paid within thirty (30) calendar days upon the insurer's receipt of documentation from the provider verifying the error.' 708(2): no duty to correct if the provider's request is filed more than 24 months after it received payment. 708(3)(a): 'Except in cases of fraud, an insurer may only retroactively deny reimbursement to a provider during the twenty-four (24) month period after the date that the insurer paid the claim'; (b) the insurer 'shall give the provider a written or electronic statement specifying the basis for the retroactive denial.' KRS 304.17A-714(1): an insurer that determines an overpayment must, within 24 months of paying the claim, give written/electronic notice of 'the amount of the overpayment, the covered person's name, patient identification number, date of service ..., insurer reference number for the claim, and the basis for determining that an overpayment exists,' and either request a refund or state that recoupment will occur if no dispute is received within 30 days; 714(2): provider disputes within 30 days by submitting additional information; 714(3): 'If a provider files a dispute ..., no recoupment shall be made until the dispute is resolved'; 714(4): disputes 'shall be processed in accordance and completed within thirty (30) days with the insurer's provider appeals process'; 714(6): at the actual time of recoupment the insurer must document amount, patient, ID and date of service. KRS 304.17A-712(1): refunds for ineligible/non-covered services must be by written notice and either refund request or 714-compliant recoupment. KRS 304.12-230 (unfair claims settlement practices) includes: '(4) Refusing to pay claims without conducting a reasonable investigation based upon all available information'; '(14) Failing to promptly provide a reasonable explanation of the basis in the insurance policy in relation to the facts or applicable law for denial of a claim or for the offer of a compromise settlement'; '(16) Knowingly and willfully failing to comply with the provisions of KRS 304.17A-714 when collecting claim overpayments from providers'; '(17) Knowingly and willfully failing to comply with the provisions of KRS 304.17A-708 on resolution of payment errors and retroactive denial of claims.' KRS 304.12-235 (general): claims must be paid 'to the named insured person or health care provider not more than thirty (30) days' after proof of claim, with 12% interest and reasonable attorney's fees where the delay 'was without reasonable foundation'; however KRS 304.17A-726 directs health claims to the 700-730 scheme, so reliance on 304.12-235 for health claims is uncertain.

**What it means for ODA:** Two detection/action rules. (A) Downcode or fee-schedule shortfall on a Kentucky-insured claim: generate a '304.17A-708(1) payment-error correction demand' (not an appeal) attaching the contracted fee schedule line and the 835 evidence; start a 30-day clock; escalate to a DOI complaint citing 304.12-230(4) and (14) if uncorrected (automated re-leveling without reviewing the record is a 'refusal to pay without reasonable investigation'). Track the 24-month lookback so the dashboard surfaces the oldest recoverable underpayments first. (B) Recoupment monitor: parse 835 PLB (WO/FB/72 etc.) and reversal/corrected-claim pairs (CLP02=22 with negative amounts); flag any recoupment more than 24 months after the original payment, any recoupment lacking the 714(1)/(6) data elements, and any recoupment taken within 30 days of notice or while a dispute is pending; auto-draft the 30-day dispute. These are also the facts a DOI complaint needs.

### [UNVERIFIED] Kentucky contract-transparency statutes give ODA the right to the payer's written payment methodology for multi-code claims (the basis of CARC 59 multiple-procedure reductions and modifier-25 edits), 90 days' advance notice of fee-schedule changes and of any 'material change' that decreases payment, 15 days' notice of changes to 'an edit program or specific edits,' and any-willing-provider protection.

KRS 304.17A-577(1)(a): an insurer issuing a managed care plan shall, upon request, provide 'the payment or fee schedules or other information sufficient to enable the health care provider to determine the manner and amount of payments under the contract' before execution or renewal; 577(1)(b): upon request the insurer shall provide 'an explanation of the methodology, such as relative value unit system and conversion factor, percentage of Medicare payment system, or percentage of billed charges, used to determine actual payment for procedures frequently performed by the provider that involve combinations of services or payment codes,' including '1. The name of any relative value system; 2. The version, edition, or publication date ...; and 3. Any applicable conversion or geographic factor'; 577(2): 'Any change to payment or fee schedules ... shall be made available to such providers at least ninety (90) days prior to the effective date of the amendment' (excluding changes in AMA codes/guidelines). KRS 304.17A-235 (formerly KRS 304.17A-578): (1)(a) 'Material change' is a change 'that decreases the health care provider's payment or compensation or changes the administrative procedures in a way that may reasonably be expected to significantly increase the provider's administrative expense'; (3) 'the insurer shall provide the participating provider with at least ninety (90) days' notice of the material change' with effective date, description, right to accept or reject, contact, and meeting opportunity; (5)(a) the provider may object in writing within 30 days, triggering a 30-day negotiation and then 30 days to unwind; (5)(b) notice must be sent 'in an orange-colored envelope with the phrase "ATTENTION! CONTRACT AMENDMENT ENCLOSED!"'; (6) 'If an insurer ... changes an edit program or specific edits, the insurer shall provide notice of the change to the participating provider at least fifteen (15) days prior to the change.' KRS 304.17A-270: 'A health insurer shall not discriminate against any provider who is located within the geographic coverage area of the health benefit plan and who is willing to meet the terms and conditions for participation established by the health insurer.' KRS 304.17A-710: insurers must disclose claims addresses, phone, delegated claims entities, with 30 days' notice of changes. KRS 304.17A-728: discounted-fee reimbursement is an unfair claims settlement practice unless the discount's products/markets are disclosed in the contract.

**What it means for ODA:** The dashboard should detect 'edit onset' events: the first remittance date on which a payer begins a new pattern (e.g., CARC 59 on 11xxx/17xxx combinations, B10 on E/M-25, G2211 denials, SVC06 code substitutions), then compare against ODA's log of payer notices. Absence of a 15-day edit notice (304.17A-235(6)) or 90-day fee-schedule/material-change notice (577(2), 235(3)) is a statutory violation that supports retroactive correction of every claim since onset. Auto-generate a 577(1)(b) methodology request for each payer applying multiple-procedure reductions (name of RVU system, version, conversion factor). Data gap: ODA must maintain a payer-notice inbox/log (orange-envelope amendments, provider bulletins) that the system can ingest; this is not in the 835 or EMA exports.

### [UNVERIFIED] Kentucky's utilization-review and internal-appeal statutes draw a line the dashboard must mirror: a claim review 'limited to an evaluation of reimbursement levels, or adjudication of payment' is not 'retrospective review,' so a code change made without a medical-necessity determination is a payment-adjudication dispute (prompt-pay/DOI track), whereas a reduction based on medical necessity is an 'adverse benefit determination' that a provider may appeal on the patient's behalf, must be decided within 30 days by a physician who was not involved in the initial decision, and leads to external review.

KRS 304.17A-600(16): 'Retrospective review' '(a) Means utilization review that is conducted after health care services have been provided to a covered person; and (b) Does not include the review of a claim that is limited to an evaluation of reimbursement levels, or adjudication of payment.' 600(1): 'Adverse benefit determination' '(a) Has the same meaning as in 29 C.F.R. sec. 2560.503-1, as amended; and (b) Includes' not-medically-necessary or experimental determinations and a coverage denial. 600(9): 'Insurer' includes '(c) Self-insurer or multiple employer welfare arrangement not exempt from state regulation by ERISA.' KRS 304.17A-617(2): 'The internal appeals process may be initiated by the covered person, an authorized person, or a provider acting on behalf of the covered person.' 617(4)(a)1: decisions 'Within thirty (30) days of receipt of the request for internal appeal' (3 business days if expedited); 617(4)(b): an internal appeal of an adverse benefit determination other than a coverage denial 'shall only be conducted by a licensed physician who did not participate in the initial review and denial,' with a board-eligible/certified specialist on request; 617(4)(c): relevant medical records considered and 'providers given the opportunity to present additional information'; 617(4)(d): determination letter must state 'the specific medical and scientific reasons for denying coverage' and, when sent to a provider, 'the medical license number of the person making the decision,' plus external-review instructions (version effective July 15, 2026). 806 KAR 17:280 Section 7(1): plans must 'Allow a covered person, authorized person, or provider acting on behalf of a covered person to request an internal appeal' within at least 60 days. The KY DOI bulletin index lists related advisory opinions by title only: AO 2020-006 'Internal and External Review Process for Self-funded Non-ERISA Plans' (10/13/2020) and AO 2023-04 'UR Medical Directors' (02/17/2023). No Kentucky statute or regulation title in Subtitle 17A or 806 KAR Chapter 17 mentions coding, bundling, downcoding or claim edits (the 806 KAR 17 index was reviewed in full).

**What it means for ODA:** Routing rule for Kentucky-insured downcodes: if the 835 shows a records request or a medical-necessity remark (e.g., CARC 50/CO-252-type documentation codes, N-series remarks about level of service) the event is an adverse benefit determination: generate a provider-on-behalf-of-patient internal appeal (30-day decision clock, demand physician reviewer license number and specific medical reasons, then external review). If no records were requested, treat it as payment adjudication and use the KRS 304.17A-702/706/708 demand plus DOI complaint track; the insurer cannot force an 'appeal' instead (304.17A-726). Kentucky has no explicit anti-downcoding statute, so Indiana's text (next findings) is the model to cite as a legislative ask. Self-funded ERISA groups are excluded from all of the above, which is why plan-funding status per group is a required data field.

### [UNVERIFIED] Indiana prompt-pay law (accident-and-sickness insurers, IC 27-8-5.7; HMOs, IC 27-13-36.2) requires a payer to notify the provider of claim deficiencies within 30 days (electronic) or 45 days (paper) or the claim is deemed clean, to pay or deny each clean claim within the same 30/45-day windows, and to pay interest on late-paid claims from day 31/46 at the state's variable rate under IC 12-15-21-3(7)(A).

IC 27-8-5.7-2: 'clean claim' means a claim submitted by a provider for payment under an accident and sickness insurance policy issued in Indiana 'that has no defect, impropriety, or particular circumstance requiring special treatment preventing payment.' IC 27-8-5.7-5(b): 'An insurer shall notify a provider of any deficiencies in a submitted claim not more than: (1) thirty (30) days for a claim that is filed electronically; or (2) forty-five (45) days for a claim that is filed on paper; and describe any remedy necessary to establish a clean claim.' 5(c): 'Failure of an insurer to notify a provider as required under subsection (b) establishes the submitted claim as a clean claim.' IC 27-8-5.7-6: the insurer must pay or deny each clean claim 'If the claim is filed electronically, within thirty (30) days after the date the claim is received by the insurer' or 'within forty-five (45) days' if on paper; if paid late, 'the insurer shall pay the provider that submitted the claim interest' on the allowable amount accruing from the 31st (electronic) or 46th (paper) day after filing through the payment date, and must 'use the same interest rate as provided in IC 12-15-21-3(7)(A).' IC 27-13-36.2-4 (HMOs): same 30/45-day pay-or-deny deadlines, interest from day 31/46, same rate reference. IC 12-15-21-3(7)(A): the rate is 'the percentage rounded to the nearest whole number that equals the average investment yield' on state general fund money for the previous fiscal year (excluding pension funds), per the state comptroller's annual financial report; the numeric rate for a given year was not retrieved. IC 27-8-5.7-7: providers may submit only CMS-1500, CMS-1450 (UB-04) or ADA claim forms. Source pages (FindLaw) state 'current as of January 1, 2026' and advise verifying with the Indiana General Assembly; the IGA site could not be rendered.

**What it means for ODA:** For Indiana-issued commercial/HMO policies the dashboard should use a 30-day (electronic) prompt-pay clock and compute interest from day 31 using an annually configured Indiana rate (open question: obtain the current percentage). Indiana's 'deemed clean' rule means any payer that pends or downcodes a claim without a timely deficiency notice has conceded it was clean. Data gap: the 835 does not reveal the policy's state of issue; map payer IDs/plan names (e.g., Anthem Indiana, Indiana-situs employer groups) to Indiana law, and note that Medicare Indiana (J8) is federal, not subject to IC 27-8-5.7.

### [UNVERIFIED] Indiana has an explicit anti-downcoding statute for both insurers and HMOs: a payer may not alter the CPT code submitted on a clean claim or pay for a lesser-value CPT code unless the submitted code violates correct-coding rules, clinical care guidelines, or the provider contract, or a human employee or contractor of the payer has reviewed the medical record; it also bars reducing a claim to only the codes needed for the final diagnosis when the billed services were medically necessary to reach that diagnosis.

IC 27-13-36.2-4.5 (HMOs), quoted verbatim: '(a) A health maintenance organization may not alter the CPT code (as defined in IC 27-1-37.5-3) submitted for a clean claim or pay for a CPT code (as defined in IC 27-1-37.5-3) of lesser monetary value unless: (1) the CPT code submitted is not in accordance with correct coding guidelines and rules, clinical care guidelines, or the terms and conditions of the participating provider's agreement or contract with the health maintenance organization; or (2) the medical record of the clean claim has been reviewed by an employee or contractor of the health maintenance organization. (b) A health maintenance organization may not alter a clean claim to only pay for the CPT codes (as defined in IC 27-1-37.5-3) necessary for an individual's final diagnosis, if the CPT codes (as defined in IC 27-1-37.5-3) billed were deemed medically necessary according to generally accepted clinical care guidelines to reach the final diagnosis. (c) This section does not prohibit a provider from appealing a claim.' IC 27-8-5.7-6.5 (accident and sickness insurers) is the parallel provision: '(a) An insurer may not alter the CPT code submitted for a clean claim' or pay a lower-value CPT code unless (1) the code does not comply with correct coding guidelines and rules, clinical care guidelines, or the provider's contract terms, or (2) an insurer employee or contractor has reviewed the medical record; (b) same final-diagnosis bundling bar; (c) does not prohibit provider appeals. Both shown 'current as of January 1, 2026' on FindLaw; enacting public-law history not retrieved.

**What it means for ODA:** Core detection rule, directly actionable for Indiana-regulated plans and persuasive everywhere: flag 'automatic downcoding' when (i) SVC06 (or the 837 submitted code) is a higher-level E/M than SVC01, or the submitted code is paid $0 and a lower code is paid, AND (ii) no medical-record request appears for that claim (no documentation-request CARC/RARC such as CO-252/N463-type codes, no 277 request, no records sent by ODA). Appeal template for Indiana plans: cite IC 27-8-5.7-6.5(a)(2) or IC 27-13-36.2-4.5(a)(2), demand the name/date of the human reviewer and the record reviewed, and state that absent review the payer must pay the submitted code. For Kentucky payers, quote the Indiana text as the standard the payer applies next door and as the legislative ask to the Kentucky General Assembly and Kentucky DOI.

Sources: [codes.findlaw.com](https://codes.findlaw.com/in/title-27-insurance/in-code-sect-27-13-36-2-4-5/) · [codes.findlaw.com](https://codes.findlaw.com/in/title-27-insurance/in-code-sect-27-8-5-7-6-5/)

### [UNVERIFIED] For self-funded ERISA plans (e.g., UMR-administered groups), a payment of less than the submitted amount, including a $0 'B10' on an E/M-25 line or a G2211 denial, is an 'adverse benefit determination' that triggers the ERISA claims rules: at least 180 days to appeal, a de novo review by a different fiduciary, free copies on request of the specific internal rule/guideline/protocol relied on and of all 'relevant' documents (including edit logic and any policy on the denied treatment), disclosure of diagnosis/treatment/denial codes and the 'standard' used, a 60-day (or 30+30-day) decision clock, and 'deemed exhaustion' with a direct right to sue if the plan does not strictly comply.

29 CFR 2560.503-1(g)(1)(v)(A): 'If an internal rule, guideline, protocol, or other similar criterion was relied upon in making the adverse determination,' the notice must give the specific rule or state that it was relied upon and that a copy will be provided free of charge upon request. (h)(2)(iii): 'Provide that a claimant shall be provided, upon request and free of charge, reasonable access to, and copies of' all relevant documents. (h)(3)(i): 'Provide claimants at least 180 days following receipt of a notification of an adverse benefit determination' to appeal. (h)(3)(ii): 'Provide for a review that does not afford deference to the initial adverse benefit determination' by a fiduciary not involved in the initial decision; (h)(3)(iii): consult 'a health care professional who has appropriate training and experience in the field of medicine.' (i)(2)(iii): post-service appeal decision 'not later than 60 days after receipt by the plan' (30 days per level if two levels). (l): if the plan fails to establish or follow compliant procedures, 'a claimant shall be deemed to have exhausted the administrative remedies available under the plan' and may sue under ERISA 502(a). (m)(8): 'relevant' includes documents relied upon, submitted/considered/generated, and any statement of policy or guidance about the denied treatment option, whether or not relied upon. (b)(4): authorized representatives may act for claimants. 29 CFR 2590.715-2719(b)(2)(ii)(E): the plan must provide 'as soon as practicable, upon request, the diagnosis code and its corresponding meaning' and the treatment code and its meaning; notices must include 'the denial code and its corresponding meaning' and 'a description of the plan's or issuer's standard, if any, that was used in denying the claim'; (b)(2)(ii)(C): the plan 'must provide the claimant, free of charge, with any new or additional evidence considered' and any new rationale before the final decision; (b)(2)(ii)(F): if a plan 'fails to strictly adhere to all the requirements of this paragraph (b)(2),' the claimant is deemed to have exhausted and may go to external review or court (narrow de minimis exception). DOL Benefit Claims Procedure FAQs: C-12 says an adverse benefit determination includes 'a failure to provide or make payment (in whole or in part) for, a benefit,' and a partial payment is adverse because 'the claimant is nonetheless receiving less than full reimbursement of the submitted expenses,' permitting a challenge to 'the plan's calculation of how much it is required to pay'; C-17: a rule that 'serves as a basis for making a benefit determination' 'must be set forth in the notice' or furnished free on request; C-16: a statement that a rule 'may have been relied upon' is insufficient; B-2: 'Typically, assignments are not a grant of authority to act on a claimant's behalf'; A-8: a provider's payment request under an assignment is treated as the claimant's claim; B-1: a plan may require a form to confirm an authorized representative.

**What it means for ODA:** For UMR/other self-funded payers the dashboard should: (1) compute an appeal deadline of remit date + 180 days (use BPR16 or the 835 production date as the notice-receipt proxy) and age each downcode/B10/G2211 event against it; (2) auto-generate an ERISA appeal that (a) invokes DOL FAQ C-12 (partial payment = adverse determination), (b) demands under 2560.503-1(g)(1)(v)(A), (h)(2)(iii) and (m)(8) the specific edit/rule (e.g., the modifier-25 or G2211 policy), the claim file, and any clinical guideline, (c) requests the diagnosis/treatment/denial codes and the 'standard' under 2590.715-2719(b)(2)(ii)(E); (3) start a 60-day response clock and flag non-response as deemed exhaustion (litigation-ready). Operational data gap: because an assignment of benefits is 'typically' not authority to act, ODA should add a patient-signed designation-of-authorized-representative to intake so the practice can run the appeal itself, and should capture each plan's funding status and any anti-assignment clause (plan documents are obtainable via the same document-request right).

Sources: [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/29/2560.503-1) · [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/29/2590.715-2719) · [dol.gov](https://www.dol.gov/agencies/ebsa/about-ebsa/our-activities/resource-center/faqs/benefit-claims-procedure-regulation)

### [UNVERIFIED] Medicare Advantage plans must pay non-contracted providers what Original Medicare would pay, must pay 95% of non-contracted clean claims within 30 days (with Medicare interest) and decide all other non-contracted claims within 60 days, may not deny basic benefits using coverage criteria other than Traditional Medicare rules or publicly posted, evidence-based internal criteria (CMS-4201-F), must have a physician or appropriate professional with knowledge of Medicare coverage criteria review any adverse medical-necessity decision, and must give the provider a written notice stating the specific reasons for any partial payment denial.

42 CFR 422.214(a)(1): a provider without a contract 'must accept, as payment in full, the amounts that the provider could collect if the beneficiary were enrolled in original Medicare'; (a)(2): Medicare's statutory payment provisions (including penalties) apply. 42 CFR 422.520(a)(1): the CMS contract 'must provide that the MA organization will pay 95 percent of the' clean claims within 30 days for non-contracted claims; (a)(2): 'The MA organization must pay interest on clean claims' not paid within 30 days per sections 1816(c)(2)(B) and 1842(c)(2)(B); (a)(3): other non-contracted claims must be paid or denied within 60 calendar days; (b)(1): provider contracts 'must contain a prompt payment provision'; (c): CMS may pay providers directly and offset the plan. 42 CFR 422.101(b)(1)-(2): MA plans must comply with 'CMS's national coverage determinations,' LCDs, and 'General coverage and benefit conditions included in Traditional Medicare laws, unless superseded by laws applicable to MA plans'; (b)(6): plans may 'create publicly accessible internal coverage criteria' 'that are based on current evidence in widely used treatment guidelines or clinical literature' only 'when coverage criteria are not fully established in applicable Medicare statutes, regulations, NCDs or LCDs,' and must post the criteria, 'a summary of evidence,' the sources, and 'An explanation of the rationale'; (c)(1)(i)(A): the plan 'may not deny coverage for basic benefits based on coverage criteria not specified in paragraph (b) or (c)'; (c)(1)(i)(C): determinations must consider 'The enrollee's medical history ..., physician recommendations, and clinical notes.' CMS fact sheet for CMS-4201-F (April 5, 2023) confirms these rules and the annual Utilization Management Committee review. 42 CFR 422.566(b)(3): an organization determination includes the plan's refusal 'to provide or pay for services, in whole or in part'; (c)(1)(ii): 'Any provider that furnishes, or intends to furnish, services to the enrollee' may request an organization determination; (d): an adverse medical-necessity decision 'must be reviewed by a physician or other appropriate health care professional' with expertise 'including knowledge of Medicare coverage criteria.' 42 CFR 422.568(d)-(e): when a plan denies payment 'in whole or in part' it must give the enrollee and provider written notice that must 'State the specific reasons for the denial' and 'Inform the enrollee of his or her right to a reconsideration'; (f): failure to give timely notice 'itself constitutes an adverse organization determination and may be appealed' (LII shows 422.568 last amended 90 FR 15910, Apr. 15, 2025, and 422.101 amended at 91 FR 17581, Apr. 6, 2026).

**What it means for ODA:** For Humana MA, UHC MA and BCBS MA: (1) benchmark every MA allowed amount (AMT*B6) against the CGS J15 (or J8 for Indiana) Medicare Physician Fee Schedule for the same code/locality/date; for non-contracted MA plans any allowed amount below the Medicare rate is a 422.214 violation and any clean claim paid after 30 days owes Medicare interest; (2) an MA plan that re-levels an E/M or denies G2211 that Traditional Medicare would pay is applying a coverage/payment criterion that must be either a Medicare rule or a publicly posted internal criterion; the appeal should demand the citation and the posted criterion under 422.101(b)(6)(ii) and the name/credentials of the 422.566(d) reviewer; (3) a remittance that reduces payment with only CAS*CO*45 and no 'specific reasons' fails 422.568(e) and the missing notice is itself appealable. Data gap: ODA's contracted vs non-contracted status with each MA plan (and each product within UHC/Humana) determines which remedies apply.

Sources: [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/42/422.214) · [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/42/422.520) · [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/42/422.101) · [cms.gov](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/2024-medicare-advantage-and-part-d-final-rule-cms-4201-f) · [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/42/422.566) · [law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/42/422.568)

### [UNVERIFIED] A non-contracted provider can appeal an MA plan's downcode or partial payment in its own name by filing a Waiver of Liability (WOL) and requesting reconsideration within 60 calendar days of receiving the determination (receipt presumed 5 days after the notice; CMS tells plans to state '65 calendar days from the remittance notification date'); CMS's current appeals guidance (effective July 6, 2026) expressly lists 'Downcoding,' 'Bundling issues and disputed rate of payment' and 'Level of care or rate of payment denials' as appealable and says the provider 'does not need to receive zero payment' to appeal. Contracted providers have no Subpart M appeal right when the enrollee owes nothing and must use the contract's dispute process.

42 CFR 422.574(b): parties to an organization determination include 'An assignee of the enrollee (that is, a physician or other provider who has furnished a service to the enrollee and formally agrees to waive any right to payment from the enrollee for that service).' 42 CFR 422.578: 'Any party to an organization determination ... may request' reconsideration. 42 CFR 422.582(b): 'a request for reconsideration must be filed within 60 calendar days' of receipt of the organization determination notice; (b)(1): receipt is presumed 5 calendar days after the notice date 'unless there is evidence to the contrary'; (c): good-cause extension if the late request states why it was late (section amended 89 FR 30827, Apr. 23, 2024).
